LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

* What India Makes of Trump’s Asia Trip
  Foreign Policy | 18 Nov 22:51 UTC
  New Delhi will watch closely as the U.S. president meets with Xi and Putin in Shenzhen.
  FULL TEXT: Welcome to Foreign Policy ’s South Asia Brief. The highlights this week: High-level diplomacy in Manila and Shenzhen captures the attention of Indian policymakers, Bangladesh is cleared to receive U.S. International Development Finance Corp. funding, and jailed former Pakistani Prime Minister Imran Khan issues a plea for medical treatment. Welcome to Foreign Policy ’s South Asia Brief. The highlights this week: High-level diplomacy in Manila and Shenzhen captures the attention of Indian policymakers, Bangladesh is cleared to receive U.S. International Development Finance Corp. funding, and jailed former Pakistani Prime Minister Imran Khan issues a plea for medical treatment. The Quadrilateral Security Dialogue held its first leaders’ meeting in more than two years on the sidelines of the Association of Southeast Asian Nations (ASEAN) summit in Manila, Philippines, on Tuesday. Australia, India, Japan, and the United States announced initiatives including cybersecurity collaboration, a cable connectivity framework, and a maritime surveillance exercise. The Quad meeting likely brought some relief for New Delhi. The grouping is important for India’s foreign-policy interests, bringing together some of its closest security partners in an effort to counter China. (However, the Quad states rarely mention Beijing in their public messaging about the grouping to avoid antagonizing the superpower.) Though India has engineered a rapprochement with China in the last two years, taking steps to reduce border tensions and scale up trade and investment, they remain bitter strategic rivals. India prioritizes opportunities to work multilaterally to push back against China’s power through non-security collaborations in areas such as technology and infrastructure investment. Scheduling issues and U.S.-India tensions have prevented high-level Quad engagement since September 2024. Quad supporters will point to Tuesday’s meeting as an indication of the grouping’s resilience, much like how it bounced back from quiescence in 2017 —interestingly, with another summit in Manila when each member’s ties with China faced increasing strain. Still, the Quad remains mired in uncertainty, mainly because of U.S. President Donald Trump. His administration’s signaling about taking a softer approach to competition with China, coupled with silence about the fate of the U.S. Indo-Pacific strategy, suggests that the current White House may not be prepared to throw its full support behind the Quad. * Congo’s Escalating Ebola Crisis
  Foreign Policy | 18 Nov 22:03 UTC
  Six months in, the “out of control” outbreak shows no sign of slowing down.
  FULL TEXT: Welcome to Foreign Policy ’s Africa Brief. The highlights this week: Health leaders issue new warnings about the Ebola outbreak in the Democratic Republic of the Congo, voters choose continuity in Cape Verde’s presidential election , and the board of an African literary prize exonerates a winner accused of using artificial intelligence. Welcome to Foreign Policy ’s Africa Brief. The highlights this week: Health leaders issue new warnings about the Ebola outbreak in the Democratic Republic of the Congo, voters choose continuity in Cape Verde’s presidential election , and the board of an African literary prize exonerates a winner accused of using artificial intelligence. Six months into the fastest-growing Ebola outbreak in history, the rare Bundibugyo variant of the virus shows no sign of slowing down in the Democratic Republic of the Congo. It is exacerbated by an underfunded health system and armed conflict in eastern provinces, where global mining projects have led to increased disease transmission. The outbreak is “still serious, and is still not under control,” said Jean Kaseya, the director-general of the Africa Centres for Disease Control and Prevention, or Africa CDC. The death toll since the outbreak began in May has passed 5,500 people, with more than 11,000 people infected, according to Congolese government figures released last week. Infections are now spreading at double the rate as when the outbreak began, according to Kaseya. More than 80 percent of new cases are emerging outside of known contact lists, and more than 65 percent of deaths have occurred outside of healthcare facilities, he added. Kaseya called the lack of coordination between Congo’s government and foreign donors “a collective failure,” urging donors to release $1.6 billion in pledged aid. International donors have disbursed more than $962 million to organizations that are implementing Congo’s Ebola response. But around $700 million of that sum was unaccounted for as of last month, according to Kaseya. The Africa CDC said that it was too early to attribute this discrepancy to embezzlement, but an investigation is underway. In 2020, Congo’s former health minister was convicted of embezzling more than $400,000 from funds intended to fight Ebola during the 2018-2020 outbreak. Meanwhile, Congolese health workers—who have not been paid for months—have gone on strike . The Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation (APEC) summit kicked off in the Chinese city of Shenzhen on Wednesday with a two-hour meeting between Xi and Putin. During the talks, Xi highlighted the importance of the China-Russia relationship and touted the necessity of a multipolar world. In a keynote address later that day, he further vowed to defy trade protectionism and maintain a “free and open” system, painting Beijing as a stable economic partner that can counter the White House’s volatile tariff war . Putin echoed that message in his own speech while criticizing Western sanctions imposed on Russia due to its war against Ukraine. The meetings and speeches did not signal a dramatic shift in policy; Xi has already used this week’s Asia-Pacific summitry to curry favor with some of the region’s most influential allies, from Canada to Peru. However, the timing was notable, as just a few hours after the Xi-Putin talks, Xi was on the tarmac at Shenzhen Baoan International Airport to personally greet Trump with a warm embrace. Such a personal welcome is uncommon for the Chinese president, who usually sends a high-level representative to meet with foreign leaders when they touch down in China.   Experts say AI likely played a role but not in the way you might think.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. A wave of tech outages in the U.S. federal government and private sector over the past week have made a lot of people understandably nervous. The outages affected commercial air travel, email, banking, and more—in some cases causing disruptions worldwide. A global Microsoft outage that began last Thursday derailed a number of services that businesses, schools, and governments rely on. Then the U.S. Federal Aviation Administration (FAA) issued a rare nationwide ground stop on Friday leading to widespread flight cancellations and delays. Chase Bank, Bank of America, and Apple services also faced brief disruptions in recent days. And on Tuesday, some internal systems at the U.S. National Security Agency (NSA) went down. A wave of tech outages in the U.S. federal government and private sector over the past week have made a lot of people understandably nervous. The outages affected commercial air travel, email, banking, and more—in some cases causing disruptions worldwide. A global Microsoft outage that began last Thursday derailed a number of services that businesses, schools, and governments rely on. Then the U.S. Federal Aviation Administration (FAA) issued a rare nationwide ground stop on Friday leading to widespread flight cancellations and delays. Chase Bank, Bank of America, and Apple services also faced brief disruptions in recent days. And on Tuesday, some internal systems at the U.S. National Security Agency (NSA) went down. Given the outages occurred on such a massive scale and in quick succession, questions have been raised as to whether they were a result of nefarious activities. But there is currently no evidence that the recent incidents were due to cyberattacks or that they were linked. The Microsoft outage was the result of a combination of factors, including a “latent bug” from July that caused servers to become “overwhelmed and unresponsive,” Charles Lamanna, Microsoft’s executive vice president of Copilot, agents, and platform, said in a post on X . Lamanna said a “surge in load” and decreased server capacity had also contributed to the outage. The FAA is still assessing what led to the ground stop, which lasted less than five hours, but a preliminary investigation indicated that the technical problem was caused by the installation of a router at a radio facility in Arkansas that may have flooded its communications network. * America and China Are Trying to Alter Their Economic Bargain
  Foreign Policy | 18 Nov 20:22 UTC
  With decoupling too painful, derisking is on the menu instead.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. The United States and China are locked in a derisking race. After decades of actively entwining their economies, both are now trying to achieve a degree of separation between them. Neither side seeks at this point to decouple completely from its rival. Each is trying instead to alter the terms of their economic relationship, reducing their own vulnerability to leverage, coercion or attacks that could damage its long-term prosperity and security, while working to acquire advantages over its counterparty. This is a struggle for power, waged through trade, investment and technology development. Today’s derisking race signals the failure of the third attempt by the United States to build an open, fully integrated world economy. At the end of the Cold War, as after the two world wars, American policymakers were motivated by a mix of pragmatism and principle. They believed that there were enormous gains to be had from trade with large and potentially fast-growing markets. But they also hoped to bring to fruition the liberal dream of a ‘one-world’ global trading system in which freer flows of goods, capital and people would promote peace and freedom, as well as prosperity. The United States and China are locked in a derisking race. After decades of actively entwining their economies, both are now trying to achieve a degree of separation between them. * ICJ to Deliver Landmark Verdict on Myanmar
  Foreign Policy | 18 Nov 15:37 UTC
  The Rohingya genocide case will have wide-ranging impacts, including for the court’s forthcoming judgment on Israel.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. The International Court of Justice (ICJ) is set to deliver a Nov. 19 verdict on whether Myanmar violated the Genocide Convention during the mass persecution and massacres of the Rohingya people that began in August 2017. The ICJ has never found a state responsible for genocide; such a verdict in this case would have immediate and wide-ranging impacts. In a span of roughly two and a half months that year, the Myanmar military killed thousands of people , committed widespread and organized sexual assault, and razed nearly 400 villages in northern Rakhine state. More than 700,000 Rohingya fled across the Naf River into Bangladesh, where many of them now remain in refugee camps. The International Court of Justice (ICJ) is set to deliver a Nov. 19 verdict on whether Myanmar violated the Genocide Convention during the mass persecution and massacres of the Rohingya people that began in August 2017. The ICJ has never found a state responsible for genocide; such a verdict in this case would have immediate and wide-ranging impacts. In a span of roughly two and a half months that year, the Myanmar military killed thousands of people , committed widespread and organized sexual assault, and razed nearly 400 villages in northern Rakhine state. More than 700,000 Rohingya fled across the Naf River into Bangladesh, where many of them now remain in refugee camps. For Rohingya victims, the ICJ case presents an opportunity to have their suffering recognized on one of international law’s biggest stages. For opponents of Myanmar’s military regime—which recently rebranded itself as a nominally civilian government—a verdict that finds the state culpable of genocide could serve as a lever to halt the regime’s recent emergence from diplomatic isolation. And in the eyes of the global pro-Palestinian movement, it would likely signal a looming genocide verdict for Israel at the same court , although that decision is not expected for a few more years. The Myanmar case is expected to set a number of international legal precedents, including those concerning standards of evidence and reparations. “From an international law perspective, this case is once in a generation,” said Michelle Onello, a senior legal advisor at the Global Justice Center, a humanitarian law organization. Gambia brought the Myanmar case to the ICJ in 2019, marking the first time that the court—which hears disputes between nations—took up a genocide case lodged by a country

The post Volunteer Dentists, the Revolutionary Guards and Rural Development on Iran’s Home Front appeared first on...
  FULL TEXT: Buses passed the burned-out metal structures of Iranian aircraft on the tarmac of Tehran’s Mehrabad Airport, remnants of the fleet Israel bombed in March this year. They were carrying passengers to two waiting Soviet-designed airlifters — an Antonov An-74 and a giant four-engine Ilyushin Il-76. The flights, which took place in late October, were conducted by the Aerospace Force of the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC). Yet the passengers consisted of more than 300 volunteer medical personnel and doctors on a health aid mission — tightly crammed on rows of benches pulled through the carrier. Less than two hours later, they had landed in Shiraz, in southern Iran. For the next three consecutive days, they spread out across six rural areas in the proximity of Shiraz, and a number of less affluent suburbs of the city, to provide medical aid. To this end, rural schools, religious congregation halls and sports halls were repurposed into makeshift clinics. Given the amount of medical equipment that needed to be transported, the endeavor was a mammoth logistical task, so the volunteers had teamed up with the IRGC Aerospace Force. The force is well known for being in charge of Iran’s missile capabilities and strategic deterrence. Yet with its fleet of airlifters and helicopters, it is also well-versed in the rapid deployment of personnel and logistics. That is a crucial but lesser-known quality of the force, and one that has come to the fore during a number of major crises since the devastating earthquake in Bam in 2003. Nearly 20 miles south of Shiraz, in Bid Zard, this delicate aspect of the cooperation was palpable. There, next to the main road, a derelict caravanserai is one of the few traces remaining of the Sassanian road network, which then connected the empire with its Mesopotamian capital. But 14 centuries later, during the 40 days of the war from late February to early April 2026, an entire residential area on the upper side of the road was ordered to evacuate as a precaution, because it lies adjacent to the outer limits of an IRGC missile facility. Fences on the surrounding hills mark its limits. The community of approximately 7,000 households witnessed and endured the horror of heavy bombings. When residents returned to their buildings, they found only shattered glass and some cracks in the plaster, though the horror remained with them. It wasn’t until deep into our wait time that we realize this is a children’s ride. (Parton was not a strict teetotaler, but the park bills itself as assertively family-friendly. “Weapons and alcoholic beverages are not permitted on Dollywood Property” is the only explanation given on its website.) * Dreams of birds
  Aeon | 18 Nov 11:01 UTC
  When bird populations decline, certain songs may be lost forever. One physicist’s mission is to revive lost melodies - by Aeon Video Watch on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: The Argentine physicist Gabriel Mindlin has spent much of his career researching how birds learn to sing. He focuses on the rufous-collared sparrow ( Zonotrichia capensis ), a species common across urban and suburban Argentina, where it’s often called a ‘chingolo’. As in other songbirds, the male’s song is not innate but passed down from adult males to younger ones, who sing it throughout their lives to guard territory and find a mate. In this short documentary, the Argentine director Martín Benchimol chronicles Mindlin’s latest breakthrough. Comparing the songs of today’s chingolos with those transcribed by hand in the 1960s, Mindlin realised that some melodies had survived the decades while others had disappeared. Using mathematical models, he and his team set out to revive the vanished songs and teach them back to living birds. With a cinematic eye, Benchimol follows the researchers as they attempt an audacious act of cultural engineering, building robotic song tutors, planting them in the wild and tracking whether young birds pick up these lost tunes. For The Atlantic , Toby Lester offers a fascinating, mildly haunting overview of the underneath—specifically, the “submerged prehistory” buried beneath the oceans, and the researchers salvaging it, bit by bit. Reading, I felt awestruck, not merely for the scale of history that lurks beneath our rising waters but the implications for the future of humanity. “What if archaeologists of the future could study only what remained above sea level, in places that today sit well inland from the coast?” an archaeologist suggests to Lester. It’s a wonder to watch Lester play with the hypothetical, to help us better grasp the depths and imagine their future reach. The chance to name a giant new Earth feature doesn’t arrive often (Antarctica, one of the most recent additions, began to appear on maps in the 1880s, although the word had been around for centuries), and if that feature happens to be largely invisible, you’re likely to run into difficulties persuading others to go along with your name. Call the feature what you will, but it exists: During the past 20,000 years, huge stretches of formerly inhabited land along the world’s coastal shelves have disappeared underwater, taking with them tens of thousands of years of human history. Until recently, that is. During the past few decades, artifacts and sites dating back millennia have been found off the coasts of every inhabited continent. A growing number of researchers—geographers, archaeologists, anthropologists, amateur enthusiasts—are turning their attention to what lies offshore. “Everywhere we’ve systematically looked, we’ve found some areas where the former landscape has survived,” Jessi Halligan, an anthropologist at Texas A&M, told me. “And in a lot of those places, those former landscapes have had sites on them.” “A new method for quantifying grandeur is reshuffling the pecking order of the planet’s most impressive peaks.   FULL TEXT: In Arugam Bay, the fishing village turned surf town on Sri Lanka’s east coast, 22-year-old Hameed, a Sri Lankan whose name has been changed at his request, was setting up his brother’s hopper stand for the evening at the tail end of the surf season. The mile-long stretch running parallel to the beach is lined with hotels, restaurants, cafes, roti stands and boutiques that stay open well into the night between May and September, but, by October, are all but abandoned. The stand is popular with tourists, Hameed told me, pointing to posters, menus and graphics around the seating area, none of which I could read because they were all in Hebrew. “99% of our customers are Israeli,” he said. “They keep coming back here. They prefer Arugam Bay over the south and the west [of the island] because the people here get along with them.” Hameed’s eldest brother, who owns the stand, was given a Hebrew name by his Israeli patrons, which he uses instead of his given Muslim name. The two brothers, who hail from the nearby town of Pottuvil, are their family’s breadwinners. Hameed got into university to study management, but did not go. “It would have taken too much time,” he said. His middle brother, a graduate, cannot find work now. So the family relies on their earnings from selling hoppers — crispy fermented pancakes with sweet and savory fillings — during Arugam Bay’s five-month surf season. Over the past decade, the area has become something of an Israeli enclave, with Israeli-owned hotels and restaurants opening at an increasing rate to cater to large groups of young Israeli tourists who, after completing mandatory service in the Israeli military, spend weeks, sometimes months, surfing and partying by the beach. Many fund their trip with savings or discharge benefits, such as a one-time release grant, and travel on a budget, staying in cheap hostels. “Some of them come here with mental issues like PTSD. They don’t want to talk about the war; they only want to relax,” a cafe worker told me. Arugam Bay and Pottuvil are majority Muslim, yet Hameed insisted the Israeli presence causes no friction, despite Israel’s ongoing aggression in Gaza and the assumption that Muslims globally stand with the Palestinian cause. “Pottuvil is 80% Muslim, but we get along well with Israelis. And they’re all funded by readers like you. Become a member today. Megan J. Arlett | Longreads | November 17, 2026 | 20 minutes (5,692 words) We live in a yellow place. Ninety miles from Roswell, New Mexico, and 120 from Lubbock, Texas, there is little in any direction but dairy farms and flat, endless plains. When I take the dogs to the sad excuse for a walking path on the edge of town (next to a field of rusted cars and the dairy plant), I can turn in a circle and see an almost 360-degree view of the horizon. Because it takes so much effort to get anywhere from here, I conceive of it as an island. There is a Walmart and a small local grocery store we call The Best Restaurant in Town because we enjoy the tamales and breakfast burritos at its hot bar. A café called Do Drop Inn serves 12 slightly different variations of a turkey sandwich. There are the staple fast-food chains of any American town: McDonald’s, Burger King, Taco Bell, Domino’s, Sonic. There is just enough to not need to leave to get anything, and not enough to make you want to stay. The quiet in Portales is like this: the big dog’s light breathing as she sleeps on the guest bed beside my desk, the little dog scratching behind her ear, the air conditioner kicking on, a distant rumble of a car down the street. In the mornings, I rarely see another person walking around the neighborhood. Sometimes, someone steps from their house and waves briefly as they get in their car, but this happens maybe once a week. Mostly it feels like I am walking around a toy town, or a town where something has gone horribly wrong and I am the last person to learn about it. Samuel Taylor Coleridge’s The Rime of the Ancient Mariner describes the doldrums as “that silent sea.” In Coleridge’s poem, the breeze drops, the sails turn slack, the sailors speak only to break the silence around them, the sun is hot and copper, and “day after day, day after day, / we stuck, nor breath nor motion: / As idle as a painted ship / Upon a painted ocean.” Sailors call it “the doldrums,” but the band of water five degrees north and south of the equator is actually the Inter-Tropical Convergence Zone. Here, where the heat of the equator forces warm air upward, there is little surface wind and the waters can stay calm for weeks. During the age of sailing, boats would languish at the ocean’s

Photo by Morteza Nikoubazl/NurPhoto/Getty Images The hijab law has been codified in Iran since 1983, enforced nationwide by the ‘morality’ police. Disobeying it meant fines, flogging or imprisonment. In December 2017, Vida Movahed, a woman in her 30s, climbed onto a utility box on Enghelab Street in Tehran, took off her white headscarf and held it up on a stick. Although she was arrested and later sentenced to a year in prison, her action proved contagious. Other women climbed up onto boxes and makeshift plinths in the same street, waving their headscarves in the air. People began calling them the Girls of Enghelab Street. They were arrested too. It is hard to convey now how much nerve their actions took. Women had been edging their scarves back for years, but taking one off in daylight, above the traffic, where the police could see their naked heads as clearly as everyone else, was something very few women had done in public, since the protests of 1979 and 1980. The not-so-subtle push comes as Turkey faces one of Europe’s steepest declines in fertility rates, despite President Recep Tayyip Erdoğan having said for nearly a decade that he wants households to have at least three children. * The small African lab sounding the alarm on AI bioweapons
  Rest of World | 16 Nov 11:00 UTC
  Photograph showing Georgina an AI Engineer building and working on the user interface of the new model Moremi.
  FULL TEXT: Out of curiosity, researchers at a small artificial intelligence lab in Accra, Ghana, removed the guardrails on their drug discovery AI model in 2024. They asked it to design toxins instead. The model produced designs for 1,020 proteins and 5,000 small molecules. The researchers never made any of them in a lab, but software that predicts how poisonous a substance is scored all of them as likely toxic. Some of them resembled ricin, a deadly poison. The study was uploaded to the preprint server arXiv in May 2025. “We were all shocked,” Darlington Akogo, founder of MinoHealth AI Labs, told Rest of World. For Akogo, the results also raised a new question: If someone took designs like these to a company that makes DNA to order, would anyone stop them? Across Africa, the need to regulate DNA synthesis providers is now echoing through the highest levels of government, Brenda Obuya, a senior official at Kenya’s Ministry of Information, Communications and the Digital Economy, told Rest of World. Akogo has traveled the world warning that AI poses an existential bioweapons threat. He discussed it on the sidelines of the World Bank Group and International Monetary Fund Spring Meetings held in Washington, D.C., last year. He discussed the broader risk over dinner with Microsoft co-founder Bill Gates in 2023, whom he described as “on the fence” at the time. This year, Gates has issued numerous public warnings. “A.I. has crossed the threshold that its ability to empower a bioterrorist to kill hundreds of millions, that exists today,” he said on The Ezra Klein Show in September. The study has been discussed within Interpol, showing just how far it has reached. “I think the fact that the Ghanaian AI lab examined this and highlighted the potential for misuse shows that African institutions are contributing to … potentially to the governance [of AI],” Adam Abdullahi, a fellow at the International Biosecurity and Biosafety Initiative for Science, told Rest of World . LLMs have become even more capable since the study came out and the barrier for entry has lowered, Ayantola Alayande, a researcher at the Global Center on AI Governance, told Rest of World. “It wouldn’t be a surprise now to see even … [lay] users generate this sort of output via chatbots.” Scholars have long warned about AI’s bioweapons threat, but it is only recently that AI systems have truly outperformed Ph.D.-level biologists . In the run up to its release, it is easy to focus on the game’s lavish variety, but to say that you can simply do a lot of things in GTA these days – sky-diving, off-road racing, manning your own taxi cab, annoying alligators – is increasingly missing the point. The point instead lies with the density of every frame: the fuzzy edges of exposed drywall in an apartment that has been hastily refitted as a drug den, the brisk splatter of blood that appears on paintwork when someone’s face collides with it. And it goes beyond these vivid details into the realms of things you may only vaguely be aware of. Fly over the mountains in GTA V and the game delivers turbulence in little bursts of haptic feedback through the controller. You may not even notice it, but afterwards you might notice its absence in other games. * The horse made the state
  Aeon | 16 Nov 11:00 UTC
  Civilisation wasn’t made possible by grain, but by transportation – and the conquest it enabled, from the steppe to the sea - by Lhamsuren Munkh-Erdene Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: A horse rider at the market place in Urga (now Ulaanbaatar), Mongolia, photographed by Stéphane Passet in July 1913. Courtesy the Musée Albert-Kahn, France Transportation is the backbone of civilisation, which is why controlling the Strait of Hormuz grants global leverage. Because oil fuels more than 90 per cent of global transit, the source of this power lies not in the waterway, but in the control of global mobility. Halting all oil supplies paralyses virtually all transport, communication and supply lines: cities collapse, states disintegrate, societies break down and populations die off, throwing humanity back to the pre-industrial age. Without transport animals, humanity would still be back in the Stone Age. No city or state can exist without transportation, for there can be no concentration of population and resources. No transportation means no extrahuman transportation, leaving walking and carrying as the only means of transit. This baseline determined the size and structure of human communities. Production was limited to what could be carried home, circumscribing the areas where humans could settle and colonise. Production capacity and resource availability kept communities small, forcing them to split and disperse. Because size dictates structure, small meant undifferentiated and egalitarian. Ultimately, the inherent limit of the physical capability of travel and portage formed a natural immunity to inequality: it kept communities small, egalitarian and autonomous, leaving their lands beyond conquest. As long as this constraint existed, communities resembled those of the Amazon Basin or the New Guinea Highlands. The domestication of animals and the invention of boats liberated humanity, making long-distance mobilisation possible. With this newfound capacity, communities no longer needed to fit their immediate environments; instead, they could build cities and integrate expansive areas – all roads lead to Rome. Transportation – not grain – is what brought the state and civilisation into existence, and what continues to maintain them. Let’s examine this power by looking from the present back to the origin of the state. Contemporary live marine traffic map available at marinetraffic.com W hoever rules the waves rules the world! Without its navy and air force, the United States cannot command global reach or project power worldwide. * Fugitive imagination
  Aeon | 13 Nov 11:00 UTC
  Wherever people are confined, displaced, silenced or made disposable, fugitive forms of knowledge and imagination emerge - by Shahram Khosravi Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Refugees at Moria Camp, Lesbos, Greece, July 2015. Photo by Georgios Makkas/Panos In 1986, I was 19 and behind bars in a small prison on the border between Iran and Pakistan. I had been arrested while attempting to cross the border irregularly into Pakistan. Everyone I met in that prison had been detained in connection with the border. It was a small space, the cells overcrowded and unsanitary, but it held an extraordinary diversity of people: local smugglers, large-scale drug-traffickers, undocumented migrant workers, and refugees; Iranians, Afghans, Pakistanis, as well as Baluch men whose communities had crossed these lands freely for generations but who were now being punished for violating the territorial order of the nation-state. Multiple languages were spoken at once. Different histories, geographies and ways of inhabiting the world converged within the same confined space. Yet we all shared one thing. We had refused, for different reasons, to respect the border. The state saw us through a single imaginary: as criminals, trespassers or illegal bodies to be contained. We, however, were animated by different imaginations. Each of us had imagined a life that exceeded the border and acted upon that imagination. In that sense, the prison did not simply contain border-crossers; it brought together people whose aspirations had collided with the territorial imaginary of the nation-state. That prison experience was perhaps one reason I became a scholar of borders. It remains, even today, the most important school of border studies I have ever attended. The conversations in the cells and during brief moments in the prison yard revolved around borders; how they operated, how they were enforced and, above all, how they might be circumvented. The prison became an unlikely site of knowledge-production: not simply the sharing of survival strategies but the enactment of an imagination that paid no heed to prison walls or state borders. M any years later, in a prison north of Stockholm, I interviewed a human smuggler who told me he saw himself as an architect: ‘I design a journey, like an architect designs a building,’ he said. The comparison stayed with me. Architects imagine and design structures that do not yet exist. Their work begins with the capacity to visualise what is not yet visible. In a similar way, smugglers design border crossings that, from the perspective of the state, would be impossible. A smuggler’s work consists of imagining routes where borders

As the home of Taiwan’s international airport as well as 2.2 million residents, Taoyuan must manage heavy truck traffic, air pollution, rapid population growth , and constant construction. Since March 2023, Taoyuan has run an AI-powered lung cancer screening initiative targeting citizens over 40 with risk factors that include occupational exposure and family history. Two years later, Taoyuan’s program had screened over 400,000 residents, detecting 233 lung cancer cases (90% in early stage), and achieving cost savings of NT$500 million ($17 million) through AI-aided diagnostics. To manage this volume without overwhelming local hospitals, the city deployed advanced AI software, which can predict cancer risk from a single low-dose CT scan without requiring additional clinical data, helping doctors to efficiently triage cases and focus resources on those most at risk. Modern AI-powered predictive models are being dangerously relied on in more decision-making arenas, including the delivery of government services, without justification. When residents complained about noise from illegally modified vehicle exhaust pipes, Taoyuan developed an AI system that analyzes exhaust pipe images and matches them against a database of legal configurations. Patrol cars equipped with cameras can now identify violations on sight, and complaints are down 20% over last year. Other AI-powered cameras monitor construction sites and factory chimneys for pollution, automatically triggering water sprays when dust levels rise too high, or alerting inspectors when chimney emissions change suspiciously. AI now optimizes traffic-light cycles at busy intersections, shaving about 10 seconds off each wait. * Why anything exists
  Aeon | 12 Nov 11:00 UTC
  Here’s a new answer to the old question ‘why is there something rather than nothing?’ (It’s neither simple nor easy) - by Andrew T Brenner Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Icebergs (1893-95) by Robert E Peary. Courtesy the Met Museum , New York Writing in 1714, Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz asked: ‘Why is there something rather than nothing?’ Philosophers have long wondered why the Universe exists, but Leibniz is credited with framing the issue in precisely these terms. The question is head-spinning when properly considered. This very obvious fact about the world – that things exist – can shift from seeming mundane and trivial to very surprising, even downright weird. Ludwig Wittgenstein expressed this astonishment when he wrote in his Tractatus (1921): ‘Not how the world is, is the mystical, but that it is.’ Arthur Schopenhauer in 1818 called the question ‘an unfathomable and always disquieting riddle’. And the Rigveda , one of the world’s oldest surviving religious texts, suggests that even the ‘overseer’ ( adhyakṣaḥ ) of the cosmos might not know the answer. Sheer existence is baffling. Philosophers continue to discuss the question today, but there isn’t any consensus about the answer. Some have suggested that it’s the sort of question that couldn’t possibly have an answer. In 1973, the German philosopher Carl Hempel wrote: His idea is that if you were trying to explain why, for instance, any penguins at all exist, then you couldn’t appeal to a penguin in your explanation – after all, that penguin is one of the things you are trying to explain. Similarly, if we’re looking for an explanation for why anything at all exists, then our explanation cannot appeal to an existing thing, since that existing thing is one of the things we are trying to explain. But Hempel thinks that there couldn’t possibly be any other sort of explanation for why anything exists, since any explanation for why something exists has to appeal to some other existing thing. One response to Hempel’s concern is to go ahead and do what he claimed can’t possibly be done: offer an explanation for why there is something rather than nothing, an explanation that ‘does not assume the existence of something’. Many philosophers have attempted to do just that. But there is something wrong with these explanations. W hy might we expect there to be an explanation for why there is something rather than nothing in the first place? Leibniz thought there must be an explanation for why there is something rather than nothing because, as he put it, nothing would have been so much ‘simpler and easier’ than something. It is this thought that lies behind much of the astonishment peo
  https://aeon.co/essays/a-new-answer-to-why-there-is-something-rather-than-nothing

Ravenna Koenig | Longreads | November 12, 2026 | 19 minutes (5,133 words) In January, gravity was the only part of the physical world I recognized as I drove through Utqiaġvik for the first time. Day was one long night that slowly paled to powder blue, and turned pink before fading to black again. It was so cold the car tires squealed over the snow as if over Styrofoam. No part of Alaska lay farther north than this and the land was stark and flat: nothing to see in the distance, and no height to see the distance from. I was there to learn about loss. For thousands of years, a collar of sea ice, in some places feet thick and miles long, had hugged this northern shore most of the year. It protected the coastline from erosion, and served as a platform from which Iñupiat hunters pursued bowhead whales and other marine mammals; had for over a thousand years. But the ice was getting thinner, forming later, melting earlier. I’d come to report on this and other ways the Arctic landscape was bending to a warming atmosphere. I introduced myself to the mayor, whaling captains, wildlife biologists, the tribal government, and corporation heads. I said hi, I’m a journalist, I cover energy and environment, I just moved to Fairbanks, I’ll be coming up throughout the year. We talked about climate change, about the wild animals that ran like gold thread through the fabric of life there, about the oil that paid for almost everything: the roads, the fire department, the light and heat. The plane had landed in darkness, and the town was full of first-story windows. But driving along the thin road that separated the plywood-sided buildings from the frozen sea, I pulled over, and climbed up a snow berm that obscured it from view. The sight stilled me. The ice was snow-covered, unmoving, unending. Only snaking bands of rubble, left behind when motion stopped, distinguished it from the tundra. I didn’t know then what it would teach me about my expectations of continuity and stillness. I didn’t know it would tell me something about how to face a disappearing world. And I didn’t know the choice I’d made to come see it—to seek meaning and adventure at this vanishing outer edge of the country—would lead to losses of other kinds, other kinds of painful education.
